p = sq + r and deg r < deg s.

Factorization of Polynomials over C (Section 4C)
Thm 4.12 (Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, first version):
Every non-constant polynomial with complex coefficients has a zero in C.
Thm 4.13 (Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, second version:):
If p ∈ P (C) is nonconstant polynomial (i.e. deg p ≥ 1), then p has a unique factorization
p(z) = c · (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm )
where c, λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ C. This factorization is unique except for the order of the factors.
Proof: Let p ∈ P (C) and put m := deg p. If m = 1, then
!
a0
p = a0 + a1 z = a1 · z −
,
a1
so the the desired factorization exists and is unique.
Hence, assume that m > 1 and that the desired factorization exists and is unique for all polynomials of degree m − 1. By the first version of the fundamental theoreom of algebra 4.12, p has a
zero λ ∈ C. By 4.6, there is polynomial q of degree m − 1 such that
p(z) = (z − λ)q(z)

∀z ∈ C.

Our induction hypothesis implies that q has the desired factorization, which when plugged into
the equation above, gives the desired factorization of p.
Uniqueness: The number c is uniquely determined as the coefficient am of zm in p. So we only
need to show that except for the order, there is only one way to choose λ1 , . . . , λm . If we have
some τ1 , . . . , τm ∈ C such that

cA · (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ) = cA · (z − τ1 ) · · · (z − τm ) ∀z ∈ C,
then because the left side of the equation above equals 0 when z = λ1 , one of the τ’s on the right

4.6. Without loss of generality, we can assume by relabeling, that τ1 = λ1 . Now, if z ̸= λ1 , we

Note that the last 2 equations hold for all z ∈ C except possibly z = λ1 . Remember that our goal
is to use induction on the degree of the polynomial. So it would come in handy if the remaining
polynomials (4.2) would be equal after cancelation and behave like any other polynomial. But
we have derived those equations by the assumption that z ̸= λ1 using division. Here comes the
trick. Let us assume that (z − λ2 ) · · · (z − λm ) and (z − τ2 ) · · · (z − τm ) are not always equal. Thus,
with the standard laws of polynomial arithmetic, we can form a new polynomial s ∈ Pm−1 (C)
with

 

s(z) := (z − λ2 ) · · · (z − λm ) − (z − τ2 ) · · · (z − τm ) ,
where we assume that s is not the zero polynomial. Using the last equation in (4.3), we know
that s(z) = 0

∀z ∈ C\{λ1 }. But we know that s can have at most m − 1 roots by 4.8. A contra-

diction, so it must be the case that s is the zero polynomial. Hence, ∀z ∈ C : s(z) = 0. The zero
polynomial is allowed to have infinitely many roots. This is only the case if ∀z ∈ C:
(z − λ2 ) · · · (z − λm ) = (z − τ2 ) · · · (z − τm ).
So, we get two identical polynomials after canceling out (z − λ1 ). By our induction hypothesis,
the equation above implies that except for the order, the λ’s are the same as the τ’s, completing
the proof of uniqueness.

Factorization of Polynomials over R (Section 4D)
Thm 4.14 (polynomials with real coefficients have nonreal zeros in pairs):
Suppose p ∈ P (C) is a polynomial with real coefficients. If λ ∈ C is a zero/root of p, then so is λ.
Proof: Let p ∈ P (C) be defined by
p(z) := a0 + a1 z + . . . + am zm

(∀z ∈ C),

where a0 , . . . , am ∈ R. Suppose λ ∈ C is a zero of p. Then
a0 + a1 λ + · · · + am λm = 0
a0 + a1 λ + · · · + am λm = 0
a0 + a1 λ + · · · + am λm = 0
m

a0 + a1 λ + · · · + am λ = 0
The equation above shows that λ is a zero of p as well.

Remark: The next result gives a factorization of a polynomial over R. The second version of the
fundamental theorem of algebra 4.13 gives a factorization of p with complex coefficients. Complex
but nonreal zeros of p come in pairs (see 4.14). Multiplying terms of the form (x − λ) and (x − λ)
together yields
(x2 − 2(Re λ)x + |λ|2 ),
which is a quadratic term of the required form. But 4.14 does not state that these two factors appear
the same number of times, as needed to make the idea work. However, the next proof works around
this point.
In the next result, either m or M may equal to 0. The numbers λ1 , . . . , λm are precisely the real zeros
of p, for these are the only real values of x for which the right side of the equation in the next result
equals 0.
Thm 4.16 (factorization of a polynomial over R):
Suppose p ∈ P (R) is non-constant. Then p has a unique factorization:
p(x) = c(x − λ1 ) · · · (x − λm )(x2 + b1 x + c1 ) · · · (x2 + bM x + cM )
where c, λ1 , . . . , λm , b1 , . . . , bM , c1 , . . . , cM ∈ R, with bk2 < 4ck

∀k ∈ {1, . . . , M}.

Proof: First we will prove that the desired factorization exists, and after that, we will prove uniqueness.
Think of p as an element of P (C), not P (R), but with real coefficients like stated in the theorem.
If all (complex) zeros of p are real, then we have the desired factorization by the second version
of the fundamental theorem of algebra 4.13. Thus suppose p has a complex zero λ ∈ C with
λ ∈/ R. By 4.14, λ is a zero of p as well. Thus we write ∀x ∈ R
p(x) = (x − λ)(x − λ)q(x)
= (x2 − 2(Re λ)x + |λ|2 )q(x),
for some q ∈ P (C), with deg q = deg p − 2. If we can prove that q has real coefficients, then using
induction on the degree of such polynomial completes the proof of the existence part of this
result. If we solve for q(x), we get
p(x)
q(x) = 2
∀x ∈ R.
x − 2(Re λ)x + |λ|2

only the zero polynomial is allowed th have infinitely many zeros. Thus all the coefficients of q

Uniqueness: A factor of p of the form x2 + bk x + ck with bk2 < 4ck can be uniquely wirtten as
(x − λk )(x − λk ) with λk ∈ C. Two different factorizations of p as an element of P (R) would lead
to two different factorizations of p as an element of P (C), contradicting using again the second
version of the fundamental theorem of algebra 4.13.

Exercises about Polynomials 4D
4

Suppose m is a positive integer. Is the set
{0} ∪ {p ∈ P (F ) : deg p = m} = S
a subspace of P (F )?
Solution: The set in question is not a subspace of P (F ), because it is not closed under addition.
Take m = 4, then p = 1 + 2x2 + 3x3 + 4x4 and s = 4x4 are both in S, but p − s is not.

5

Is the set
{0} ∪ {p ∈ P (F ) : deg p is even} = S
a subspace of P (F )?
Solution: The same counter-example as in the previous question applies to this one.

Invariant Subspaces (Section 5A)
Eigenvalues (Sub-Section 5A1)
Def 5.1 (operator):
A linear mapfrom a vector spaceto itself is called an “operator”.
(Suppose T ∈ L(V ), then may be T V is not an operator on a subspace Vk )
k

Def 5.2 (invariant subspace):
Let T ∈ L(V ). A subspace U of V is called “invariant under T ” if ∀u ∈ U : T u ∈ U .
Thus U is invariant under T if T U is an operator on U .
Example 5.3:
Let T ∈ L(P (R)) such that T p = p′ . Let U = P4 (R) ⊆ P (R). Then U is invariant under T because if
p ∈ U , deg p = 4 and deg(p′ ) = 3.
Example 5.4:
Let T ∈ L(V ). Then {0}, V , null T , range T are all invariant.
(Sometimes, null T = {0} and range T = V if T is invertible.)
Invariant subspaces of dimension one:
Take any v ∈ V , v ̸= 0 and let
U :≡ {λv | λ ∈ F } = span(v),
then U is a one-dimensional subspace of V .
If U is invariant under an operator T ∈ L(V ), then T v ∈ U . =⇒ ∃λ ∈ F : T v = λv.
Conversely if T v = λv, λ ∈ F , then span(v) is a one-dimensional subspace of V invariant under
T.
Def 5.5 (eigenvalue):
Suppose T ∈ L(V ). A scalar λ ∈ F is called an “eigenvalue of T ” if there exists a nonzero (i.e., v ̸= 0)
v ∈ V such that
T v = λv.
Thm 5.7 (equivalent conditions to be an eigenvalue):
The following are equivalent for T ∈ L(V ) and λ ∈ F .
(a) λ is an eigenvalue of T .
(b) T − λI is not injective.
(c) T − λI is not surjective.
(d) T − λI is not invertible.
Proof: Conditions (a) and (b) are equivalent because the eigenvector v is a solution to T v = λv,
which is equivalent to (T − λI)v = 0. Hence, dim null(T − λI) > 0, which implies that T − λI is
not injective. (b), (c) and (d) are therefore equivalent by 3.65.
Def 5.8 (eigenvector):
Let T ∈ L(V ). A vector v ∈ V is called an “eigenvector” of T corresponding to λ if v ̸= 0 and T v = λv.
In other words: A vector v ∈ V , v ̸= 0 is an eigenvector corresponding to λ ⇐⇒ v ∈ null(T − λIV )

Invariant Subspaces (Section 5A)

Thm 5.11 (linearly independent eigenvectors):
Every list of eigenvectors of T corresponding to distinct eigenvalues of T is linearly independent.
Proof: Suppose the desired result is false. Then there exists a smallest list of length m of linearly
dependent eigenvectors v1 , . . . , vm with distinct eigenvalues λ1 , . . . , λm . Since an eigenvector is
unequal to the zero vector, m must be ≥ 2.
Because of the minimality of m and because our list is linearly dependent:
∃a1 , . . . , am , where not all a’s are equal to 0, such that a1 v1 + · · · + am vm = 0.
Now we apply (T − λm I) on both sides of the equation and get
(a1 λ1 v1 − a1 λm v1 ) + · · · +
(am−1 λm−1 vm−1 − am−1 λm vm−1 ) + (am λm vm − am λm vm ) = 0

| {z }
| {z }

̸=0

By the minimality of m, the list v1 , . . . , vm−1 is linearly independent. Hence a1 (λ1 − λm ) = · · · =
am−1 (λm−1 − λm ) = 0. Since all eigenvalues are distinct, this forces a1 = · · · = am−1 = 0. Returning
to the original equation yields am vm = 0, which implies am = 0 (since vm ̸= 0), contradicting that
not all coefficients were zero. Therefore, no such linearly dependent list of eigenvectors can
exist.
Thm 5.12 (number of eigenvalues):
Each operator T ∈ L(V ) on V has at most dim V distinct eigenvalues.
Proof: Let T ∈ L(V ). Suppose λ1 , . . . , λm are distinct eigenvalues with corresponding eigenvectors
v1 , . . . , vm ∈ V . Hence, Theorem 5.11 implies that the list v1 , . . . , vm is linearly independent.
Thus, ?? implies that m ≤ dim V .

Thm 5.18 (null space and range of p(T ) are invariant under T ):
For every T ∈ L(V ), and every p ∈ P (F ),
null p(T ) and range p(T ) are invariant under T .
Proof: Step 1: First, let u ∈ null p(T ). Then,
p(T )u = 0.
Since p(T ) commutes with T (by associativity and distributivity of polynomial evaluation),
(p(T ))(T u) = (p(T )T )(u)
= (T p(T ))(u)
= T (p(T )u)
= T (0) = 0.
Hence, T u ∈ null p(T ).
Step 2: Second, let u ∈ range p(T ). Then, u = p(T )v for some v ∈ V . Thus,
T u = T (p(T )v) = p(T )(T v),
so, T u ∈ range p(T ).

Exercises about Invariant Subspaces 5A
1

Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and U is a subspace of V . Then
(a) U ⊆ null T =⇒ U is invariant under T .
(b) range T ⊆ U =⇒ U is invariant under T .
Proof: (a). If v ∈ U , then T v = 0 ∈ U , since U is a subspace and therefore 0 ∈ U .
(b). Let w ∈ U ⊆ V . =⇒ T w ∈ range T , and since range T ⊆ U , T w ∈ U .

2

Suppose that T ∈ L(V ) and V1 , . . . , Vm are subspaces of V invariant under T . Prove that V1 +
· · · + Vm is invariant under T .
Proof: Let w ∈ V1 + · · · + Vm such that w = v1 + · · · + vm where v1 ∈ V1 , . . . , vm ∈ Vm . Then
T w = T v1 + · · · + T vm such that T v1 ∈ V1 , . . . , T vm ∈ Vm because V1 , . . . , Vm are invariant under T .
Therefore we have T w ∈ V1 + · · · + Vm . So V1 + · · · + Vm is invariant under T as well.

3

Suppose T ∈ L(V ). Prove that the intersection of every collection of subspaces of V invariant
under T is invariant under T .
Proof: Let w ∈ V1 ∩ · · · ∩ Vm ⊆ V where each Vi is invariant under T . Then w ∈ V1 , . . . , w ∈ Vm ,
and thus, T w ∈ V1 , . . . , T w ∈ Vm . Hence, T w ∈ V1 ∩ · · · ∩ Vm .

12 Suppose V = U ⊕W , where U and W are nonzero subspaces of V . Define P ∈ L(V ) by P (u +w) =
u for each u ∈ U and each w ∈ W . Find all eigenvalues and eigenvectors of P .
Solution: We have an eigenvalue λ1 = 1 for all vectors u ∈ U , because P acts as the identity on
U . We also have an eigenvalue λ0 = 0 for all w ∈ W , because P acts as the zero operator there.

22 Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and there exist nonzero vectors u, w ∈ V such that
T u = 3w

and

T w = 3u

Prove that 3 or −3 is an eigenvalue of T
Solution: If T is defined as above, then T (u + w) = 3w + 3u = 3(u + w). Hence, u + w gets sent to
3(u + w), which makes 3 an eigenvalue of T . If we change the definition of T to
T (u) = 3(−w)

and

T (−w) = 3u,

then we have
T (u + w) = T (u + (−1)(−1)w) = T (u) − T (−w) = −3w − 3u = −3(u + w).
Thus, u + w gets sent to −3(u + w) and −3 is an eigenvalue of T as well. Note that we didn’t
prove that those are the only eigenvalues.

Invariant Subspaces (Section 5A)

KI Bemerkung: Hier standen zwei fehlerhafte bzw. unvollständige Beweisversuche. Ich habe
diese durch einen korrekten und eleganten Beweis über den dualen Operator ersetzt, der auch
das Ergebnis von Aufgabe 15 direkt ausnutzt.
Proof: ⇒ direction: Suppose T has an eigenvalue λ ∈ F . By Exercise 15, λ is also an eigenvalue
of the dual operator T ′ ∈ L(V ′ ). Thus, there exists a nonzero linear functional ϕ ∈ V ′ such that
T ′ (ϕ) = λϕ. This means that for every u ∈ V , we have ϕ(T u) = (T ′ ϕ)(u) = (λϕ)(u) = λϕ(u).
Let U = null ϕ. Because ϕ ̸= 0, the Fundamental Theorem of Linear Maps tells us that dim U =
dim V − dim(range ϕ) = dim V − 1. If u ∈ U , then ϕ(u) = 0, so ϕ(T u) = λϕ(u) = 0, which implies
T u ∈ null ϕ = U . Hence, U is a subspace of dimension dim V − 1 that is invariant under T .
⇐ direction: Suppose there exists a subspace U ⊆ V of dimension dim V − 1 that is invariant
under T . Let U 0 ⊆ V ′ be the annihilator of U . Since dim U = dim V − 1, we know that dim U 0 =
dim V − dim U = 1. Because U is invariant under T , its annihilator U 0 is invariant under the
dual operator T ′ . Since U 0 is a one-dimensional subspace invariant under T ′ , any nonzero
functional ϕ ∈ U 0 is an eigenvector of T ′ . Hence, T ′ has an eigenvalue. By Exercise 15, T must
also have an eigenvalue.
25 Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and u, w are eigenvectors of T such that u + w is also an eigenvector of T .
Prove that u and w are eigenvectors of T corresponding to the same eigenvalue.
Proof: Let u, w ∈ V \ {0} such that T u = λu u, T w = λw w, and T (u + w) = λu+w (u + w). Since
T (u + w) = T u + T w = λu u + λw w = λu+w (u + w) = λu+w u + λu+w w,
it follows that




λu − λu+w u + λw − λu+w w = 0.
If u and w are linearly independent, the coefficients must vanish, so λu = λu+w = λw . Otherwise,
if u and w are linearly dependent, we can write u = αw for some α ̸= 0. Applying T to u in two
ways gives
T u = T (αw) = αT w = αλw w and
T u = λu u = λu αw.
Hence, λu = λw .

33 Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and m is a positive integer.
(a) Prove that T is injective ⇐⇒ T m is injective.
(b) Prove that T is surjective ⇐⇒ T m is surjective.
Proof: We show (a) and (b) both with induction, first in the ⇒ direction, and then in the ⇐
direction.
(a) ⇒ direction: Suppose T is injective. For m = 1 the statement is trivial (T 1 = T ). Fix m ≥ 1
and suppose as induction hypothesis that T m is injective. If T m+1 (v1 ) = T m+1 (v2 ), then
T (T m (v1 )) = T (T m (v2 )).
Since T is injective, T m (v1 ) = T m (v2 ). The induction hypothesis forces v1 = v2 . Therefore,
T m+1 is injective as well. (In general, function composition is injective. If f : X → Y and
g : Y → Z are injective, so is g ◦ f : X → Z. So, a finite composition of injective maps is
injective.)
⇐ direction: Assume T m is injective for some fixed m ≥ 1. If m = 1 we are done. If
64

Invariant Subspaces (Section 5A)

m > 1, take v1 , v2 ∈ V with T (v1 ) = T (v2 ). Apply T m−1 to both sides to get T m−1 (T (v1 )) =
T m−1 (T (v2 )). Injectivity of T m forces v1 = v2 . Thus, T is injective.
(b) ⇒ direction: Suppose T is surjective. For m = 1 the statement is trivial. Fix m ≥ 1 and
assume that T m is surjective. Let y ∈ V be arbitrary. Since T is surjective, there exists z ∈ V
with T (z) = y. By the induction hypothesis, there exists v ∈ V with T m (v) = z. Thus,
T m+1 (v) = T (T m (v)) = T (z) = y.
Hence, T m+1 is surjective.
(Or: A finite composition of surjective maps (not necessarily linear) is surjective.)
⇐ direction: Assume T m is surjective for some fixed m ≥ 1. Let y ∈ V be arbitrary. Choose
v ∈ V with T m (v) = y (always possible, since T m is surjective). Put x := T m−1 (v). Then
y = T (x) = T (T m−1 (v)) = T m (v).
Hence, T is surjective.

The Minimal Polynomial (Section 5B)
Existence of Eigenvalues on Complex Vector Spaces (Sub-Section 5B1)
Thm 5.19 (existence of eigenvalues):
Every operator on a finite-dimensionalnonzero, complex vector space has an eigenvalue.
Proof: Suppose V is finite-dimensional with dim V ≥ 1 and T ∈ L(V ). Let v ∈ V be nonzero. Then
the list
v, T v, T 2 v, . . . , T n v
of length n + 1 cannot be linearly independent. Hence, some non-trivial linear combination
of these vectors is zero, so there exists a non-constant polynomial p ∈ P (C) of minimal degree
such that
p(T )v = 0.
By the first version of the fundamental theorem of algebra 4.12, there exists a root ∃λ ∈ C with
p(λ) = 0.
By 4.6, we know that ∃q ∈ P (C) such that we can factor out (z − λ) and write
p(z) = (z − λ)q(z)

∀z ∈ C.

Applying this to T gives
0 = p(T )v = (T − λI)(q(T )v).
If q(T )v were zero, then q (of strictly smaller degree) would also annihilate v, contradicting the
minimality of p. Thus
q(T )v ̸= 0
and so λ is an eigenvalue of T with eigenvector q(T )v.

Eigenvalues and the Minimal Polynomial (Sub-Section 5B2)
Def 5.20 (monic polynomials):
A “monic polynomial” is a polynomial whose highest-degree coefficient equals 1.
Example 5.21:
p(z) = 2 + 9z2 + z7 ,

deg p = 7.

Thm 5.22 (existence, uniqueness, and degree of minimal polynomial):
Suppose T ∈ L(V ). Then there exists a unique monic polynomial p ∈ P (F ) of smallest degree such
that
p(T ) = 0.
Furthermore, deg p ≤ dim V .
Proof: Before beginning our inductive proof, we examine some base cases. We first prove the existence of a monic polynomial that annihilates T and later establish its uniqueness; these two
parts are independent.
Base case: If dim V = 0, then V = {⃗0}, and the identity operator I is the zero-operator on V . We
let p(z) := 1 so that
p(T ) = I = 0.
Another base case: Now suppose that dim V = 1. For any nonzero v ∈ V , the list v, T v is
66

The Minimal Polynomial (Section 5B)

linearly dependent (since it has length 2), so there exists a scalar c0 ∈ F such that
c0 v + T v = 0.
This follows from the linear dependence lemma Theorem 2.19. If we choose q(z) := c0 + z

∀z ∈

F , then
q(T )v = (c0 I + T )(v) = c0 v + T v = 0.
In the case where T is the zero operator, one may instead choose q(z) = z. So we have dim null q(T ) =
1 and therefore dim range q(T ) = dim V −dim null q(T ) = 1−1 = 0. Thus, every vector in this onedimensional space is a scalar multiple of v and maps to zero by q(T ). Thus, q(z) serves as a
minimal polynomial for T .
Yet another base case: Suppose dim V = 2. For any nonzero v ∈ V , the list v, T v, T 2 v is linearly
dependent (since it has length 3). According to the linear dependence lemma Theorem 2.19,
we either have
c0 v + T v = 0 or c0 v + c1 T v + T 2 v = 0,
for some scalars c0 , c1 ∈ F . If we define q(z) := c0 + z in the first case or q(z) := c0 + c1 z + z2 in the
second case accordingly, then q(T )v = 0.
In the first case where (−1)c0 v = T v and q(z) = c0 +z, the vector v by itself is linearly independent
and thus dim null q(T ) ≥ 1. Therefore
dim range q(T ) ≤ dim V − dim null q(T ) ≤ 1.
Since range q(T ) is at most one-dimensional and is invariant under T (by Theorem 5.18), we
can restrict T to this subspace. In this restricted setting range q(T ), we know how to construct
the desired polynomial; denote it by s, defined such that


s T range q(T ) = 0.
Just for clarity, the equation above means that s is the zero-operator of T range q(T ) . Therefore,


∀u ∈ V : (sq)(T ) (u) = s(T )(q(T )u) = 0.
Thus, sq is a candidate for our minimal polynomial of T .
A similar reasoning goes for the case where (−1)c0 v + (−1)c1 T v = T 2 v and q(z) = c0 + c1 z + z2 .
Here we have v and T v as linearly independent vectors. Note that if k ∈ N, then
q(T )(T k v) = T k (q(T )v) = T k (0) = 0.

(5.1)

Hence the equation (5.1) above tells us that v ∈ null q(T ) and T v ∈ null q(T ). Here in this case
we conclude that dim null q(T ) ≥ 2 and therefore dim range q(T ) = 0. Since dim null q(T ) = 2, we
have that
null q(T ) = V .
This means that every vector of V gets mapped to 0 if q(T ) is applied. So we have found a
minimal monic polynomial!
General case: As we can see a pattern now, we can use induction on dim V and assume dim V >
2 and that the theorem holds for all vector spaces of smaller dimension than dim V . Let v ∈ V
be nonzero. Then the list
v, T v, . . . , T dim V

(5.2)

(which has length 1 + dim V ) is linearly dependent. By the linear dependence lemma (Theorem 2.19), there is a smallest positive integer m ≤ dim V and some c0 , . . . , cm−1 ∈ F such that
c0 v + c1 T v + · · · + cm−1 T m−1 v + T m v = 0.
67

The Minimal Polynomial (Section 5B)

Let us define q ∈ Pm (F ) as
q(z) := c0 + c1 z + · · · + cm−1 zm−1 + zm
accordingly. Thus, we have
q(T )v = 0.
Note that q(z) is a monic polynomial and that deg q = m. Observe that if k ∈ N, then
q(T )(T k v) = T k (q(T )v) = T k (0) = 0.

(5.3)

By the linear dependence lemma (Theorem 2.19) and the definition of m, the list
v, T v, . . . , T m−1 v

(5.4)

from before is linearly independent (This is not the same list as (5.2) since m ≤ dim V ). This
fact together with equation (5.3) imply that the vectors of the list (5.4) all belong to null q(T ).
Hence,
dim null q(T ) ≥ m.
Using the Rank-Nullity Theorem, this implies:
dim range q(T ) = dim V − dim null q(T ) ≤ dim V − m.
Because range q(T ) is invariant under T by Theorem 5.18, we can apply our induction hypothesis to the operator T range q(T ) . Hence, there exists a monic polynomial s ∈ P (F ) such that
deg s ≤dim V − m and

s T range q(T ) = 0.
In other words, ∀u ∈ range q(T ) : s(T )(u) = 0. So we can safely say that


s(T ) range q(T ) = s T range q(T ) .
Because ∀u ∈ V : q(T )u ∈ range q(T ), we conclude:


∀u ∈ V : (sq)(T ) (u) = s(T )(q(T )u) = 0.
Therefore, sq is a monic polynomial such that
deg sq ≤ (dim V − m) + m = dim V
and (sq)(T ) = 0.
Proof of uniqueness: Let p ∈ P (F ) be a monic polynomial of smallest degree such that
p(T ) = 0.
Let r ∈ P (F ) be another monic polynomial of the same degree such that r(T ) = 0. This implies
(p − r)(T ) = 0.
Moreover, deg(p − r) < deg p = deg r. If p − r ̸= 0, we could divide p − r by the coefficient of
the highest order term in p − r to obtain a monic polynomial that, when applied to T , yields
the zero-operator. This polynomial would have a smaller degree than p or r, which would be a
contradiction. Therefore, p − r = 0 ⇐⇒ p = r.
Def 5.24 (minimal polynomial):
Let T ∈ L(V ). The “minimal polynomial of T ” is the unique monic polynomial p ∈ P (F ) of smallest
degree such that p(T ) = 0.
Computation of the minimal polynomial: Find the smallest m ∈ N such that:
c0 I + c1 T + · · · + cm−1 T m−1 = −T m has a solution c0 , . . . , cm−1 ∈ F . Solve for m = 1, 2, . . . , dim V
Even faster (usually), pick v ∈ V with v ̸= 0 and consider the equation
c0 v + c1 T v + · · · + cdim V −1 T dim V −1 v = −T dim V v.
68

The Minimal Polynomial (Section 5B)

If this equation has a unique solution, as happens most of the time,
c0 , c1 , c2 , . . . , cdim V −1 , 1
are the coefficients of the minimal polynomial of T .
Thm 5.27 (eigenvalues are the zeros of the minimal polynomial):
Let T ∈ L(V ), which means T is an operator and let V be finite-dimensional (dim v ̸= ∞). Then
(a) The zeros of the minimal polynomial of T are the eigenvalues of T .
(b) If V is a complex vector space, the minimal polynomial has the form
(z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ),
where λ1 , . . . , λm are the eigenvalues of T , possibly with repetitions (may be λi = λj for i ̸= j).
Proof: Let p be the minimal polynomial of T .
(a) ⇒ direction: Suppose λ ∈ F is a zero of p. Hence, we can write p as p(z) = (z − λ)q(z),
where q is a monic polynomial with coefficients in F (see 4.6). As we can write
p(T ) = 0,
we can also write
0 = (T − λI)(q(T )v)

∀v ∈ V .

Because q is of lesser degree than p (i.e. deg q = (deg p)−1) and p is the minimal polynomial
of T , there exists at least one vector u ∈ V such that
q(T )u ̸= 0,
which makes q(T )u an eigenvector with eigenvalue λ.
⇐ direction: Conversely, suppose λ ∈ F is an eigenvalue of T . Thus, there exists a
nonzero u ∈ V such that T u = λu. Repeated applications of T on both sides of this equation show that
T k u = λk u

∀k ∈ N.

This implies that
p(T )u = p(λ)u.
Because p is the minimal polynomial of T , we have p(T )u = p(λ)u = 0. Since u is nonzero,
we have p(λ) = 0. This makes λ a zero of p.
(b) use (a) and the second version of the fundamental theorem of algebra (4.13).
Thm 5.29 (every “zero-polynomial” is a multiple of the minimal polynomial):
Let T ∈ L(V ) and q ∈ P (F ). Then q(T ) = 0 ⇐⇒ q is a multiple of the minimal polynomial of T .
Or equivalently: The minimal polynomial of T divides every polynomial q for which q(T ) = 0 (sometimes written as mT | q).
Proof: Let p denote the minimal polynomial of T .
⇒ direction: Suppose q(T ) = 0. By (4.9) there exists s, r ∈ P (F ) such that
q = ps + r,

deg r < deg p.

We have
0 = q(T ) = p(T )s(T ) + r(T ) = r(T ).

(5.5)

The equation above implies that r = 0. Otherwise, dividing r by its highest-degree coefficient would produce a monic polynomial that when applied to T gives 0. A contradiction
because deg r < deg p and p is minimal. Thus, (5.5) becomes the equation q = ps, as desired

69

The Minimal Polynomial (Section 5B)

⇐ direction: Suppose q = ps for q, p, s ∈ P (F ). We have
q(T ) = p(T )s(T ) = 0s(T ) = 0,
as desired. Which proves both directions.
Thm 5.31 (minimal polynomial of a restriction operator):
If U is a subspace of V , then the minimal polynomial of T is a polynomial multiple of the minimal
polynomial of T U .
Proof: Suppose p is the minimal polynomial of T ∈ L(V ) and U ⊆ V . Then
p(T )v = 0

∀v ∈ V .

In particular,
p(T )u = 0 ∀u ∈ U .


Thus p T U = 0. Now, the previous theorem 5.29 tells us that p is a polynomial multiple of
the minimal polynomial of T U . [This means that the degree of p is at least as large].
Thm 5.32 (invertibility and the constant term of the minimal polynomial):
Let T ∈ L(V ). Then
T is not invertible ⇐⇒ the constant term of the minimal polynomial of T is 0.
Proof: T is not invertible ⇐⇒ (5.7) 0 is an eigenvalue of T ⇐⇒ (5.27) 0 is a zero of p ⇐⇒ the
constant term of p is 0. (In the first equivalence, we have actually used that 0 is an eigenvalue
of T if and only if T − 0I is not invertible, according to 5.7.)

Eigenvalues on Odd-Dimensional Real Vector Spaces (Sub-Section 5B3)
Thm 5.33 (even-dimensional null space):
Suppose F = R and V is finite-dimensional. Suppose also that T ∈ L(V ) and b, c ∈ R with b2 < 4c.
Then dim null(T 2 + bT + cI) is an even number.

the fact that w ∈/ U . Since U was assumed to be maximal among even-dimensional S-invariant
subspaces, the existence of U + W , with dim(U + W ) = dim U + 2, contradicts the maximality of
U . Thus, the assumption that U ̸= N was incorrect. Therefore, we conclude that U = N . Hence,
N = null(S 2 + bS + cI) = null(T |2N +bT |N +cI) = null(T 2 + bT + cI) has even dimension dim N .
Thm 5.34 (operators on odd-dimensional vector spaces have eigenvalues):
Every operator on an odd-dimensional vector space has an eigenvalue.
Proof: Suppose F = R and V is finite-dimensional. Let n := dim V , and suppose n is an odd number.
Let T ∈ L(V ). We will use induction on n in steps of size two to show that T has an eigenvalue.
Note that the desired result holds if dim V = 1, because then every nonzero vector in V is an
eigenvector of T .
Now, suppose n ≥ 3 and the desired result holds for all operators on all odd-dimensional vector
spaces of dimension less than n, i.e. with dimension ≤ n − 2. Let p denote the minimal polynomial of T . If p is a polynomial multiple of x − λ for some λ ∈ R, then λ is an eigenvalue of T by
theorem 5.27 and we are done. Thus, we can assume that there exists b, c ∈ R such that b2 < 4c
and p is a polynomial multiple of x2 + bx + c by 4.16. By looking at this theorem, we can see that
there exists a monic polynomial q ∈ P (R) such that p(x) = q(x)(x2 + bx + c)

∀x ∈ R. Now,

2

0 = p(T ) = (q(T ))(T + bT + cI),
which means that q(T ) equals 0 on range(T 2 + bT + cI). Or with other words,
q(T )|range(T 2 +bT +cI) = 0.
Because deg q < deg p and p is the minimal polynomial of T , this implies that
range(T 2 + bT + cI) ̸= V .

(5.7)

The rank-nullity theorem 3.21 tells us that
dim V = dim null(T 2 + bT + cI) + dim range(T 2 + bT + cI)

(5.8)

The previous theorem 5.33 tells us that dim null(T 2 + bT + cI) is even. Since dim V is odd, the
equation (5.8) above and equation (5.7) shows that dim range(T 2 + bT + cI) is odd as well and
at least 2 less than dim V . We know that range(T 2 + bT + cI) is invariant under T by 5.18. Our
induction hypothesis now implies that T restricted to range(T 2 + bT + cI), which is a subset of
71

The Minimal Polynomial (Section 5B)

V , has an eigenvalue; this also means that T has an eigenvalue.

Exercises about the Minimal Polynomial 5B
1

Suppose T ∈ L(V ). Prove that 9 is an eigenvalue of T 2 ⇐⇒ 3 or −3 is an eigenvalue of T .
Proof: ⇐ direction: Suppose −3 is an eigenvalue of T . Thus, there exists v ∈ V such that T v =
−3v. If we apply T a second time, we get T T v = T (−3v) = −3T v = (−3)(−3)v = 9v. Therefore, 9
is an eigenvalue of T 2 . The case where we start with 3 as an eigenvalue of T works analogously.
⇒ direction: Let v ̸= 0 satisfy T 2 v = 9v. Then
0 = (T 2 − 9I)v = (T − 3I)(T + 3I)v.
Set w := (T + 3I)v. Then (T − 3I)w = 0, so T w = 3w (because w ∈ null(T − 3I)). There are two
cases:
• If w = 0, then (T + 3I)v = 0. Hence, T v = −3v and −3 is an eigenvalue of T .
• If w ̸= 0, then w is an eigenvector of T with eigenvalue 3.
Thus, T has eigenvalue 3 or −3, as required.

4

Suppose F = C, T ∈ L(V ), p ∈ P (C), and α ∈ C. Prove that α is an eigenvalue of p(T ) ⇐⇒
α = p(λ) for some eigenvalue λ of T .
Proof:
⇐ direction: Suppose λ ∈ C is an eigenvalue of T with α = p(λ). Then there exists a nonzero
v ∈ V such that T v = λv. Repeated application of T yields T k v = λk v for all k ∈ N, and therefore
p(T )v = p(λ)v = αv. Since v ̸= 0, α is an eigenvalue of p(T ).
⇒ direction: Conversely, suppose α is an eigenvalue of p(T ). Consider the polynomial q(z) :=
p(z) − α ∈ P (C). Since F = C, by the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra (second version), q(z)
factors completely into linear factors over C:
p(z) − α = c(z − λ1 )(z − λ2 ) · · · (z − λm ),
for some non-zero scalar c ∈ C and λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ C. Substituting T into this polynomial equation
gives:
p(T ) − αI = c(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I) · · · (T − λm I).
Since α is an eigenvalue of p(T ), the operator p(T )−αI is not injective (and hence not invertible).
A product of linear maps is non-invertible if and only if at least one factor is non-invertible.
Therefore, there exists some j ∈ {1, . . . , m} such that T − λj I is not injective. This means λj is an
eigenvalue of T . Evaluating the polynomial identity at z = λj yields:
p(λj ) − α = c(λj − λ1 ) · · · (λj − λj ) · · · (λj − λm ) = 0 =⇒ α = p(λj ).
Setting λ := λj completes the proof.

12 Define T ∈ L(F n ) by T (x1 , x2 , x3 , . . . , xn ) = (x1 , 2x2 , 3x3 , . . . , nxn ). Find the minimal polynomial
of T .
Solution: For a standard basis e1 , . . . , en we have T (e1 + · · · + en ) = e1 + 2e2 + · · · + nen . Or equivalently:
T (e1 ) = e1
T (e2 ) = 2e2
..
.
T (en ) = nen .
Hence, e1 , . . . , en are dim F n = n distinct eigenvectors with corresponding eigenvalues 1, . . . , n.
This makes T e1 , . . . , T en linearly independent as well. Using Theorem 5.27, we conclude that

the minimal polynomial of T looks like this:
p(z) = (z − 1)(z − 2) · · · (z − n)

∀z ∈ F .

We can check if ∀v ∈ F n , given as v = a1 e1 + · · · + an en (ai ∈ F ), the following equation holds:
(T − I)(T − 2I) · · · (T − nI)v = 0.
This is true because ak ek ∈ null(T − kI) for k = 1, . . . , n. Therefore,
(T − I)(T − 2I) · · · (T − nI)v = (T − I)(T − 2I)(T − 3I) · · · (T − nI)(a1 e1 )

Thm 5.39 (conditions for upper-triangular matrix):
If T ∈ L(V ) and v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V , then the following are equivalent.
(a) The matrix M(T ) of T with respect to v1 , . . . , vn is upper triangular.
(b) span(v1 , . . . , vk ) is invariant under T for every k = 1, . . . , n.
(c) T vk ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vk ) for every k = 1, . . . , n.

Hence, the upper-triangular structure of M(T ) implies
T vj ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vj ).

∀k ∈ {1 . . . n}.

that there exist ak , . . . , an ∈ F , such that
T vk = a1 v1 + · · · + ak vk = a1 v1 + · · · + ak vk + 0vk+1 + · · · + 0vn .
Hence, all the entries below the diagonal of M(T ) are 0, because the list v1 , . . . , vn is linearly
independent and we cannot choose another representation for T vk in respect to that list. Thus,
M(T ) is an upper-triangular matrix, completing the proof that (c) implies (a). After all, we
have shown that (a) =⇒ (b) =⇒ (c) =⇒ (a).
Thm 5.40 (equation satisfied by operator with upper-triangular matrix):
Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and V has a basis with respect to which T has an upper-triangular matrix M(T )
with diagonal entries λ1 , . . . , λn . Then
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λn I) = 0.

Then

(5.11)

which implies that
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)v1 = 0,
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)(T − λ3 I)v1 = 0,
. . . or more generally speaking:
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm I)v1 = 0, for m = 1, . . . , n.
(Using commutativity for linear maps after multiplying both sides of the equation with anything.)
Note that (T − λ2 I)v2 ∈ span(v1 ) because T v2 = A1,2 v1 + λ2 v2 . Thus using (5.11)
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)v2 = (T − λ1 I)(A1,2 v1 )
(5.12)

= A1,2 (T − λ1 I)v1
= 0,
which implies that
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)(T − λ3 I)v2 = 0,
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)(T − λ3 I)(T − λ4 I)v2 = 0,
. . . or more generally speaking:
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm I)v2 = 0, for m = 2, . . . , n.
(Using commutativity of polynomial factors of T .)
Note that (T − λ3 I)v3 ∈ span(v1 , v2 ) because T v3 = A1,3 v1 + A2,3 v2 + λ3 v3 .
Thus using (5.11) and (5.12), we get
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)(T − λ3 I)v3 = (T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)(A1,3 v1 + A2,3 v2 )
= A1,3 (T − λ2 I)(T − λ1 I)v1 + A2,3 (T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I)v2
= A1,3 (T − λ2 I)(0) + A2,3 (0)
= 0,
which implies that
(T − λ1 I)(T − λ2 I) · · · (T − λm I)v3 = 0, for m = 3, . . . , n.
Continuing this pattern, we see that
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λn I)vk = 0

∀k ∈ {1 . . . n}.

Thus (T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λn I) is the 0 operator because it is 0 on each vector in a basis of V .

Thm 5.41 (determination of eigenvalues from upper-triangular matrix):
Suppose T ∈ L(V ) has an upper-triangular matrix with respect to some basis of V . Then the eigenvalues of T are precisely the entries on the diagonal of that upper-triangular matrix.
Proof: Let v1 , . . . , vn denote a basis of V with respect to which T has an upper-triangular matrix with
diagonal entries

Because T v1 = λ1 v1 , we see that λ1 is an eigenvalue of T . Suppose k ∈ {2, . . . , n}.
Then (T − λk I)vk ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vk−1 ), because
T vk = M(T )1,k v1 + M(T )2,k v2 + · · · + M(T )k−1,k vk−1 + λk vk .
Thus T − λk I maps span(v1 , . . . , vk ) into span(v1 , . . . , vk−1 ).
Because
dim span(v1 , . . . , vk ) = k and dim span(v1 , . . . , vk−1 ) = k − 1,
this implies that the restriction of T − λk I to span(v1 , . . . , vk ) is not injective by Theorem 3.22.
Thus
∃v ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vk ), v ̸= 0 such that (T − λk I)v = 0.
Thus, λk is an eigenvalue of T with eigenvector v. Hence we have shown that every entry on
the diagonal of M(T ) is an eigenvalue of T .
To prove T has no other eigenvalues, let
q(z) :≡ (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λn ).
=⇒ q(T ) = 0 by Theorem 5.40
Hence q is a polynomial multiple of the minimal polynomial of T by Theorem 5.29. Thus
every zero of the minimal polynomial (i.e. every eigenvalue of T ) is a zero of q, which means
every eigenvalue of T is in {λ1 , . . . , λn }. Because the zeros of the minimal polynomial of T are
the eigenvalues of T by 5.27, this implies that every eigenvalue of T is a zero of q. Hence the
eigenvalues of T are all contained in the list λ1 , . . . , λn .

(5.15)

T vk = T vk − λm vk + λm vk = (T − λm I)vk + λm vk .

(5.16)

(5.16) above shows that

(5.17)

matrix with respect to the basis u1 , . . . , uM , v1 , . . . , vN of V .
Thm 5.47 (sufficient condition about the field for every operator on V to have an upper-triangular matrix):
Let F = C. Let T ∈ L(V ), dim V ̸= ∞. Then T has an upper-triangular matrix with respect to some
basis of V .
Proof: This follows from the previous theorem and 5.44 and the second version of the fundamental
theorem of algebra 4.13. Because F = C, the minimal polynomial of T can be expressed as
p ∈ P (C), where
p(z) := (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm )

∀z ∈ C.

Hence, T has an upper-triangular matrix M(T ).

Exercises about Upper-Triangular Matrices 5C
10 Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V . Show that the following are equivalent.
(a) The matrix M(T ) of T with respect to v1 , . . . , vn is lower triangular.
(b) span(vk , . . . , vn ) is invariant under T for each k = 1, . . . , n.
(c) T vk ∈ span(vk , . . . , vn ) for each k = 1, . . . , n.

Diagonalizable Operators (Section 5D)
Diagonal Matrices (Sub-Section 5D1)
Def 5.48 (diagonal matrix):
A “diagonal matrix” is a square matrix that is 0 everywhere except possibly on the diagonal.
Def 5.50 (diagonalizable):
An operator on V is called “diagonalizable” if the operator has a diagonal matrix with respect to some
basis of V .
Def 5.52 (eigenspace, E(λ, T )):
Let T ∈ L(V ) and λ ∈ F . The “eigenspace” of T corresponding to λ is the subspace E(λ, T ) of V defined
by
E(λ, T ) :≡ null(T − λI) = {v ∈ V | T v = λv}.

Suppose T ∈ L(V ) and λ1 , . . . , λm are distinct eigenvalues of T . Then
E(λ1 , T ) + · · · + E(λm , T )

Conditions for Diagonalizability (Sub-Section 5D2)
Thm 5.55 (conditions equivalent to diagonalizability):
Let λ1 , . . . , λm denote the distinct (i.e., λj ̸= λk ) eigenvalues of T ∈ L(V ). Then the following are
equivalent:
(a) T is diagonalizable.
(b) V has a basis consisting of eigenvectors of T .
(c) V = E(λ1 , T ) ⊕ · · · ⊕ E(λm , T ).
(d) dim V = dim E(λ1 , T ) + · · · + dim E(λm , T ).
Proof: We prove (a) ⇐⇒ (b), (b) =⇒ (c), (c) =⇒ (d), and then (d) =⇒ (b). Let λ1 , . . . , λm be the
distinct eigenvalues of T ∈ L(V ) for the whole proof.
Step 1: Suppose (a) holds. The matrix of T is diagonal with respect to some basis v1 , . . . , vn
(where n = dim V ). This means that each column contains only a diagonal entry, which is the
80

Diagonalizable Operators (Section 5D)

case ⇐⇒ T vk = λk vk for each k = 1, . . . , n. Thus v1 , . . . , vn are eigenvectors of T . Thus (a) and
(b) are equivalent, i.e. (a) ⇐⇒ (b).
Step 2: Now, suppose (b) holds; thus V has a basis consisting of eigenvectors of T . Hence, every
vector in V is a linear combination of such basis, which implies
V = E(λ1 , T ) + · · · + E(λm , T ).

Theorem 2.38 (n = dim V ). Now, we have shown that (d) =⇒ (b), completing the proof.
Thm 5.58 (enough eigenvalues implies diagonalizability):
If T ∈ L(V ) has dim V distinct eigenvalues, then T is diagonalizable.
Proof: Let λ1 , . . . , λdim V be the distinct eigenvalues of T with corresponding eigenvectors v1 , . . . , vdim V ,
which are linearly independent by Theorem 5.11. So we have a basis consisting of dim V eigenvectors. Hence, T is diagonalizable by Theorem 5.55.
Thm 5.62 (necessary and sufficient condition for diagonalizability):
Let T ∈ L(V ), dim V ̸= ∞. Then T is diagonalizable ⇐⇒ the minimal polynomial of T equals
(z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ),
where λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ F and λi ̸= λj for i, j ∈ {1, . . . , m}.
Proof: For this proof, let T ∈ L(V ) be finite-dimensional (dim V ̸= ∞).
⇒ direction: Suppose T is diagonalizable. Thus, there exists a basis v1 , . . . , vn of V consisting
of eigenvectors of T . Let λ1 , . . . , λm be said distinct eigenvalues of T . Therefore, for every vj we
81

Diagonalizable Operators (Section 5D)

have (T − λk I)vj = 0. Thus,
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm I)vj = 0,
which implies the minimal polynomial equals (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ). (Why?)
⇐ direction: Suppose the minimal polynomial of T equals (z−λ1 ) · · · (z−λm ) for some λ1 , . . . , λm ∈
F with λi ̸= λj . Write
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm−1 I)(T − λm I) = 0.

(5.20)

Suppose m = 1. Then T − λ1 I = 0 ⇐⇒ T = λ1 I, so T is diagonalizable. This is our base case
for induction over m ∈ N. Now, suppose m > 1 that and the desired result holds for all values
smaller than m. Like in the proof of theorem 5.44, we observe that range(T − λm I) is invariant
under any polynomial of T (using 5.18 with p(z) := z − λm ). Now, define
U := range(T − λm I).
This means that T U is an operator on U . If u ∈ range(T − λm I), then u = (T − λm I)v for some
v ∈ V . Together with (5.20), we have that
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm−1 I)u = (T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm I)v

(5.21)

= 0.
Since u ∈ U was arbitrary, q(z) := (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm−1 ) is a polynomial such that q(T )(u) = 0 for
every u ∈ U . By 5.29, q(z) is a polynomial multiple of the minimal polynomial of T U . By our
induction hypothesis, there is a basis of U consisting of eigenvectors of T U and therefore, as
well as eigenvectors of T . Suppose
x ∈ U ∩ null(T − λm I) = U ∩ E(λm , T ).
Hence, T x = λm x, because T x − λm Ix = (T − λm I)x = 0. Since x ∈ U , equation (5.21) implies that
0 = (T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm−1 I)x
= (λm I − λ1 I) · · · (λm I − λm−1 I)x
= (λm − λ1 ) · · · (λm − λm−1 )x
Because ∀i ̸= j : λj ̸= λk , this can only happen if x = 0. Therefore,
U ∩ null(T − λm I) = {0}.
Thus, U + null(T − λm I) is a direct sum by 1.46. That is,
U + null(T − λm I) = U ⊕ null(T − λm I)
Since (T − λm I) ∈ L(V ) we can use the Rank Nullity Theorem 3.21 to conclude that
dim V = dim U + dim null(T − λm I).
3.94 states that a sum is a direct sum ⇐⇒ the dimensions add up to the dimension of the sum.
This means that we can rewrite the equation above to
dim V = dim(U ⊕ null(T − λm I))
According to 2.39, together with the fact that U ⊕ null(T − λm I) ⊆ V , this implies
V = U ⊕ null(T − λm I) = U ⊕ E(λm , T ).
Every vector in null(T − λm I) is an eigenvector of T with eigenvalue λm . Earlier in this proof we
saw that there is basis of U = range(T −λm I) consisting of eigenvectors of T (using our induction
hypothesis on this smaller subspace of V ). Adjoining to that basis a basis of E(λm , T ) = null(T −
λm I) gives a basis of V consisting of eigenvecors of T . The matrix M(T ) of T with respect to
this basis is a diagonal matrix, as desired, using 5.55 (according to that theorem, V has a basis
consisting of eigenvectors of T ∈ L(V ) ⇐⇒ it is diagonalizable). As a side node, this theorem
states that V = E(λ1 , T ) ⊕ · · · ⊕ E(λm , T ), which correlates with V = U ⊕ null(T − λm I).
82

Diagonalizable Operators (Section 5D)

Thm 5.65 (restriction of diagonalizable operator to invariant subspace):
Let T ∈ L(V ). Suppose T is diagonalizable and U is a subspace of V that is invariant under T . Then
T U is a diagonalizable operator on U
Proof: Diagonazability of T ⇐⇒ (5.62) the minimal polynomial of T equals
(z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ) for λj ̸= λk .
By 5.31, the minimal polynomial of T is a polynomial multiple of the minimal polynomial of
T U . Hence the minimal polynomial of T U has the form required by 5.62, which shows that
T U is diagonalizable. It consists of any factor of the form (z − λk ).

Exercises about Diagonalizable Operators 5D
2

Suppose T ∈ L(V ) has a diagonal matrix A = M(T ) with respect to some basis of V . Prove that
if λ ∈ F , then λ appears on the diagonal of A precisely dim E(λ, T ) times.
KI Bemerkung: Es gab hier drei verschiedene, teilweise fehlerhafte oder unvollständige Beweise für dieselbe Aufgabenstellung (die zudem als Exercise 2 und 3 dupliziert war). Ich habe
diese zusammengefasst und den besten, ausführlichsten Beweis behalten sowie die Makros (wie
\linmap statt \op) vereinheitlicht.
Proof: If λ∗ is not an eigenvalue of T , then
dim E(λ∗ , T ) = dim{v ∈ V : T v = λ∗ v} = dim{0} = 0,
and therefore λ∗ does not appear among the diagonal entries of A.
Now assume λ∗ is an eigenvalue. Let B = {v1 , . . . , vℓ } be a basis of V with respect to which
A = M(T ) is diagonal; write
A = diag(µ1 , . . . , µℓ ),
so that for each j we have T vj = µj vj . In particular every vj is an eigenvector of T (possibly for
different eigenvalues).
Define the subset of basis vectors corresponding to the eigenvalue λ∗ by
S = { vj ∈ B | µj = λ∗ }.
We first observe that span(S) ⊆ E(λ∗ , T ), since each vector in S is by definition a λ∗ -eigenvector
and linear combinations of λ∗ -eigenvectors remain in the λ∗ -eigenspace.
For the reverse inclusion, let w ∈ E(λ∗ , T ). Express w in the basis B:

reverse inclusion and therefore
span(S) = E(λ∗ , T ).

Diagonalizable Operators (Section 5D)

Since S is a subset of the basis B, its vectors are linearly independent; hence the number of
elements of S equals the dimension of its span. Therefore
|S|= dim(span(S)) = dim E(λ∗ , T ).
Finally, by definition |S| is exactly the number of diagonal entries of A equal to λ∗ . We conclude
that the scalar λ∗ appears on the diagonal of A precisely dim E(λ∗ , T ) times.

Def 6.1 (dot product):
The “dot product” of x, y ∈ Rn is defined by
x · y :≡ x1 y1 + · · · + xn yn
The dot product on Rn has the following properties.
• x · x = ∥x∥2

∀x ∈ Rn

• x · x = 0 ⇐⇒ x = 0
• y ∈ Rn : The map from Rn to R that sends x ∈ Rn to x · y is linear.
T : Rn → R, T (x) = x · y. Then T is linear for a fixed y ∈ Rn .
• x · y = y · x.
For z = (z1 , . . . , zn ) ∈ Cn , we define the norm of z by

Def 6.2 (inner product):
The “inner product” on V is a function that takes each ordered pair (u, v) of elements of V to a number
⟨u , v⟩ ∈ F and has the following properties:
• Positivity: ⟨v , v⟩ ≥ 0

∀v ∈ V

• Definiteness: ⟨v , v⟩ = 0 ⇐⇒ v = 0
• Additivity in first slot: ⟨u + v , w⟩ = ⟨u , w⟩ + ⟨v , w⟩
• Homogeneity in first slot:

λu , v = λ ⟨u , v⟩

∀u, v, w ∈ V

∀λ ∈ F and ∀u, v ∈ V .

• Conjugate symmetry: ⟨u , v⟩ = ⟨v , u⟩ ∀u, v ∈ V
Example 6.3:
⟨w , z⟩ :≡ c1 w1 z1 + · · · + cn wn zn (for positive constants c1 , . . . , cn ).
Def 6.4 (inner product space):
An “inner product space” is a vector space V along with an inner product on V .
Thm 6.6 (basic properties of an inner product):
∀u, v, w ∈ V and ∀λ ∈ F :
(a) For each fixed u ∈ V , the map x ∈ V 7→ ⟨x , u⟩ ∈ F is a linear map from V to F .
(b) ⟨0 , v⟩ = 0
(c) ⟨v , 0⟩ = 0
(d) ⟨u , v + w⟩ = ⟨u , v⟩ + ⟨u , w⟩
(e) u , λv = λ ⟨u , v⟩

Def 6.10 (orthogonal):
u, v ∈ V are called “orthogonal” or “perpendicular” if
⟨u , v⟩ = 0. One also writes u ⊥ v.
Thm 6.11 (orthogonality and 0):
(a) 0 is orthogonal to every vector in V .
(b) 0 is the only vector that is orthogonal to itself.

Thm 6.17 (triangle inequality):
Suppose u, v ∈ V . Then
∥u + v∥ ≤ ∥u∥ + ∥v∥ .
This inequality is an equality ⇐⇒ one of u, v is a non-negative real multiple of the other (i.e., u = cv
with c ≥ 0 or v = cu with c ≥ 0).

Every orthonormal list of vectors is linearly independent.

Def 6.27 (orthonormal basis):
An “orthonormal basis” of V is an orthonormal list of vectors in V that is also a basis of V .
Thm 6.28 (orthonormal lists of the right length are orthonormal bases):
Suppose V is finite-dimensional. Then every orthonormal list of vectors in V of length dim V is an
orthonormal basis of V .
Proof: By Theorem 6.25, every orthonormal list is linearly independent. Every linearly independent
list in V of length dim V is a basis of V (by Theorem 2.38). Hence the list is an orthonormal
basis of V .

Orthogonal Complements and Minimization Problems (Section 6C)
Orthogonal Complements (Sub-Section 6C1)
Def 6.46 (orthogonal complement, U ⊥ ):
If U ⊂ V , then the “orthogonal complement” of U , denoted by U ⊥ , is defined as follows:
U ⊥ :≡ {v ∈ V | ⟨u , v⟩ = 0

∀u ∈ U }.

Thm 6.48 (properties of the orthogonal complement):
(a) If U ⊆ V , then U ⊥ is a subspace of V .
90

Orthogonal Complements and Minimization Problems (Section 6C)

(b) {0}⊥ = V .
(c) V ⊥ = {0}.
(d) If U is a subset of V , then U ∩ U ⊥ ⊆ {0}.
(e) If G and H are subsets of V and G ⊆ H, then
H ⊥ ⊆ G⊥ . This is not a typo.
Proof:
(a) We have to check for additive identity, closedness under addition and closedness under
scalar multiplication.
• ⟨u , 0⟩ = 0

∀u ∈ U =⇒ 0 ∈ U ⊥ .

• Let v, w ∈ U ⊥ , u ∈ U . Then ⟨u , v + w⟩ = ⟨u , v⟩ + ⟨u , w⟩ = 0 + 0 = 0 =⇒ v + w ∈ U ⊥ .
• Let λ ∈ F , w ∈ U ⊥ , u ∈ U . Then u , λw = λ ⟨u , w⟩ = λ0 = 0 =⇒ λw ∈ U ⊥ .
Therefore U ⊥ is a subspace of V .
(b) Let v ∈ V . =⇒ ⟨0 , v⟩ = 0 =⇒ v ∈ {0}⊥ . Since v was arbitrary, {0}⊥ = V .
(c) Let v ∈ V ⊥ =⇒ ⟨v , v⟩ = 0 =⇒ v = 0 =⇒ V ⊥ = {0}.
(d) Suppose U ⊆ V and u ∈ U ∩ U ⊥ =⇒ ⟨u , u⟩ = 0 =⇒ u = 0 =⇒ U ∩ U ⊥ ⊆ {0}.
(e) Suppose G ⊆ H ⊆ V .
Let v ∈ H ⊥ =⇒ ⟨u , v⟩ = 0

∀u ∈ H

=⇒ ⟨u , v⟩ = 0

∀u ∈ G

=⇒ v ∈ G⊥ . Thus H ⊥ ⊆ G⊥ .

Suppose U is a finite-dimensional subspace of V . Then
U = (U ⊥ )⊥
Proof: We proof this theorem by showing that U ⊆ (U ⊥ )⊥ and (U ⊥ )⊥ ⊆ U .
=⇒ ⟨u , w⟩ = 0 ∀w ∈ U ⊥ . Therefore for the same w’s using conjugate

symmetry

Thm 6.54:
Suppose U is a finite-dimensional subspace of V . Then
U ⊥ = {0} ⇐⇒ U = V .

V.
“⇐-direction:” Only using 6.48 (c): If U = V , then U ⊥ = V ⊥ = {0}.

Minimization Problems (Sub-Section 6C2)
Given a subspace U ⊆ V and v ∈ V , find a u ∈ U such that ∥v − u∥ is as small as possible.
Thm 6.58 (minimizing distance to a subspace):
Suppose U is a finite-dimensional subspace of V , v ∈ V , and u ∈ U . Then
v − PU v ≤ ∥v − u∥ .
Furthermore, the inequality above is an equality ⇐⇒ u = PU v.
Proof: For this proof, visualize that v − PU v is pointing from PU v to v and PU v − u is pointing from
u to the projection of v, namely PU v which lies on the vector space U .
We also have the properties that v − PU v ∈ U ⊥ and PU v − u ∈ U . Therefore, we can use the
Pythagorean theorem Theorem 6.12 after adding a always-positive term to the left hand side
of the inequality.
v − PU v

Pseudoinverse (Sub-Section 6C3)
If T ∈ L(V , W ) is not invertible, instead of solving the equation T v = w, which is the same as T v − w =
0, we can try to find v ∈ V such that T v − w is as small as possible.
Thm 6.59 (restriction of a linear map to obtain a one-to-one and onto map):
Suppose dim V ̸= 0 and T ∈ L(V , W ). Then T (null T )⊥ is an injective map of (null T )⊥ onto range T .
Proof: We will first prove injectivity and then surjectivity. Suppose v ∈ (null T )⊥ such that T (null T )⊥ =
0. Hence T v = 0 and v ∈ (null T ) ∩ (null T )⊥ , which implies that v = 0 by Theorem 6.48, because
(null T ) ∩ (null T )⊥ ⊆ {0}.
=⇒ null T (null T )⊥ = {0},
which Theorem 3.15 implies that T (null T )⊥ is injective.
To prove that range T (null T )⊥ is onto range T , we will show that range T (null T )⊥ ⊆ range T and
then range T ⊆ range T (null T )⊥ . Because this will imply that range T = range T (null T )⊥ and
therefore surjectivity.
Proof of range T (null T )⊥ ⊆ range T : The fact that range T (null T )⊥ ⊆ range T is actually obvious,
because range T (null T )⊥ is just T on a restricted domain (null T )⊥ .
Proof of range T ⊆ range T (null T )⊥ : Now suppose w ∈ range T =⇒ ∃v ∈ V suchthatw = T v.

93

Orthogonal Complements and Minimization Problems (Section 6C)

By Theorem 6.49, there exists u ∈ null T and x ∈ (null T )⊥ such that
v = u + x ⇐⇒ x = v − u.
=⇒ T (null T )⊥ x = T (x) = T (v − u) = T (v) − T (u) = T (v) − 0 = w,
which shows that w ∈ range T (null T )⊥ and thus range T ⊆ range T (null T )⊥ .
We conclude: range T = range T (null T )⊥ .

Thm 7.5 (properties of the adjoint):

(f) If T is invertible, then T ∗ is invertible and (T ∗ )−1 = (T −1 )∗ .

Def 7.7 (conjugate transpose, A∗ ):
For A ∈ F m,n we define its “conjugate transpose” A∗ ∈ F n,m like follows:
If j ∈ {1, . . . , n} and k ∈ {1, . . . , m} then
(A∗ )j,k :≡ Ak,j .
If a matrix has only real entries, then
A∗ = At .

Self-Adjoint Operators (Sub-Section 7A2)
Def 7.10 (self-adjoint):
An operator T ∈ L(V ) is called “self-adjoint” if
T = T ∗ ⇐⇒ T v , w = v , T w

∀v, w ∈ V .

If F = R, then every eigenvalue is real, so the next result is interesting only when F = C.
Thm 7.12 (eigenvalues of self-adjoint operators):
Every eigenvalue of a self-adjoint operator is real.
Proof: Suppose T is a self-adjoint operator on V . Let λ be an eigenvalue of T , and let v ∈ V , v ̸= 0
such that T v = λv. Then
λ ∥v∥2 = λv , v = T v , v = v , T v = v , λv = λ ∥v∥2 .
Thus λ = λ, which means that λ is real, as desired.

Normal Operators (Sub-Section 7A3)
Def 7.18 (normal):

• An operator on an inner product space is called “normal” if it commutes

with its adjoint.
• In other words, T ∈ L(V ) is normal if T T ∗ = T ∗ T .
Every self-adjoint operator is normal, because if T ∗ = T , then T commutes with T ∗ .
Example 7.19 (an operator that is normal but not self-adjoint):
Let T be the operator on F 2 whose matrix with respect to the standard basis is

This operator T is not self-adjoint because M(T )2,1 = 3 which is not equal to the complex conjugate
of the entry in M(T )1,2 = −3.
Nonetheless, for the matrix of T T ∗ we have

Because T T ∗ and T ∗ T have the same matrix, we see that T T ∗ = T ∗ T . Thus T is normal.

Thm 7.24 (invertible quadratic expressions):

Thm 7.27 (minimal polynomial of self-adjoint operator):
Suppose T ∈ L(V ) is self-adjoint. Then the minimal polynomial of T equals (z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ) for
some λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ R.
Proof: First suppose F = C. The zeros of the minimal polynomial of T are the eigenvalues of T by
5.27 (a). Because T is self-adjoint, all eigenvalues of T are real by 7.12. Thus the second version
of the fundamental theorem of algebra 4.13 tells us that the minimal polynomial of T has the
desired form.
Now suppose F = R. By 4.16 there exist λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ R and b1 , . . . , bN , c1 , . . . , cN ∈ R with bk2 <
4ck ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , N } such that the minimal polynomial of T equals
(z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ) · (z2 + b1 z + c1 ) · · · (z2 + bN z + cN ).

(7.3)

Note that it might be the case that m = 0 or N = 0, meaning that there are not terms of the
corresponding form. If N = 0, we are done with this proof. Now
(T − λ1 I) · · · (T − λm I) · (T 2 + b1 T + c1 I) · · · (T 2 + bN T + cN I) = 0
If N > 0, then we could multiply both sides of the equation above on the right by the inverse
(T 2 + bN T + cN I)−1 of T 2 + bN T + cN I, which is an invertible operator by 7.24. We would then
obtain a polynomial expression of T that equals 0. The corresponding polynomial would have
a degree 2 less than the degree of (7.3), violating the minimality of the minimal polynomial.
Thus we must have N = 0, which means that the minimal polynomial in (7.3) has the form
(z − λ1 ) · · · (z − λm ), as desired.
104

Spectral Theorem (Section 7B)

The previous result along with 5.27 implies that every self-adjoint operator has an eigenvalue. The
next result will show that self-adjoint operators have enough eigenvalues to form a basis. It gives a
complete description of self-adjoint operators on a real inner product space and is one of the major
theorems in linear algebra.
Thm 7.28 (real spectral theorem):
Suppose F = R and T ∈ L(V ). Then the following are equivalent.
(a) T is self-adjoint.
(b) T has a diagonal matrix with respect to some orthonormal basis of V .
(c) V has an orthonormal basis consisting of eigenvectors of T .
Proof: Step 1: First, suppose (a) holds, so T is self-adjoint. Our results on minimal polynomials,
specifically
• Every orthonormal list extends to an orthonormal basis (Todo: 6.37)
• minimal polynomial of self-adjoint operator
imply that T has an upper-triangular matrix with respect to some orthonormal basis of V . With
respect to this basis, the matrix M(T ∗ ) of T ∗ is the transpose of the matrix M(T ) of T :
M(T ∗ ) = M(T )t .
However, T ∗ = T . Thus M(T )t = M(T ). Because M(T ) is upper-triangular, this means that all
entries of the matrix above and below the diagonal are 0. Hence M(T ) is a diagonal matrix with
respect to the orthonormal basis. Thus (a) implies (b).
Step 2: Second, suppose (b) holds, so T has a diagonal matrix M(T ) with respect to some
orthonormal basis of V . That diagonal matrix M(T ) equals its transpose (i.e. M(T ) = M(T )t ).
Thus, with respect to that basis, T ∗ = T , proving that (b) implies (a).
Step 3: The equivalence of (b) and (c) follows from the definitions. Suppose (b) has a diagonal
matrix whose entries are λ1 , . . . , λm with an orthonormal basis e1 , . . . , em . Hence, T ek = λk ek (for
k = 1, . . . , m). Therefore, e1 , . . . , em are the eigenvectors of T .

Thm 7.29 (complex spectral theorem):

(a) T is normal. (T T ∗ = T ∗ T )
(b) T has a diagonal matrix with respect to some orthonormal basis of V .
(c) V has an orthonormal basis consisting of eigenvectors of T .

thus T commutes with T ∗ , which means that T is normal. In other words, (a) holds, completing
the proof that (b) =⇒ (a).

Positive (Semidefinite) Operators (Section 7C)
Def 7.34 (positive (semidefinite) operators):
An operator T ∈ L(V ) is called “positive (semidefinite)”, if T is self-adjoint and
T v ,v ≥ 0

∀v ∈ V .

One can either use the term “positive operator” or “positive semidefinite operator”, which means the
same.
Def 7.36 (square root):
An operator R is called “square root” of an operator T if R2 = T .
Thm 7.38 (characterizations of positive operators):
Let T ∈ L(V ). Then the following are equivalent.
(a) T is a positive operator.
(b) T is self-adjoint and all eigenvalues of T are nonnegative
(c) With respect to some orthonormal basis of V , the matrix M(T ) of T is a diagonal matrix with
only nonnegative numbers on the diagonal.
(d) T has a positive square root.
(e) T has a self-adjoint square root.
(f) T = R∗ R for some R ∈ L(V ).
106

Isometries, Unitary Operators, and Matrix Factorization (Section 7D)

Proof: We will prove (a) ⇒ (b) ⇒ (c) ⇒ (d) ⇒ (e) ⇒ (f ) ⇒ (a)
Step 1: If (a) holds, that means T is self-adjoint and T v , v ≥ 0 for all v ∈ V . Let λ ∈ F be an
eigenvalue of T with eigenvector v ̸= 0. Then
0 ≤ T v , v = λv , v = λ ⟨v , v⟩ = λ ∥v∥2 =⇒ λ ≥ 0.
Thus (b) holds.

By (b), each λk ≥ 0. So (b) =⇒ (c).

Isometries, Unitary Operators, and Matrix Factorization (Section 7D)
Isometries (Sub-Section 7D1)
Def 7.42 (isometry):
S ∈ L (V , W ) is called an “isometry” if
Sv = ∥v∥

∀v ∈ V .

In other words, a linear map is an isometry if it preserves norms.

107

Isometries, Unitary Operators, and Matrix Factorization (Section 7D)

If S ∈ L(V , W ) is an isometry and v ∈ V is such that Sv = 0, then
∥v∥ = Sv = ∥0∥ = 0,
which implies that v = 0. Thus every isometry is injective.
(The Greek word isos means “equal”; the Greek word metron means “measure”. Thus isometry
literally means “equal measure”.)
Thm 7.49 (characterizations of isometries):
Suppose S ∈ L(V , W ). Suppose e1 , . . . , en is an orthonormal basis of V and f1 , . . . , fm is an orthonormal
basis of W . Then the following are equivalent.
(a) S is an isometry.
(b) S ∗ S = I.
(c) Su , Sv = ⟨u , v⟩

∀u, v ∈ V .

(e) The columns of M(S, (e1 , . . . , en ), (f1 , . . . , fm )) form an orthonormal list in F m with respect to the
Euclidean inner product.
Proof: We will prove (a) ⇒ (b) ⇒ (c) ⇒ (d) ⇒ (e) ⇒ (a)

Unitary Operators (Sub-Section 7D2)
Def 7.51 (unitary operator):
An operator S ∈ L(V ) is called “unitary” if S is an invertible isometry.
Note: Every isometry is injective. Every injective operator on a finite-dimensional vector space is
invertible. So “unitary” and “isometry” mean the same thing for operators on finite-dimensional
108

Isometries, Unitary Operators, and Matrix Factorization (Section 7D)

inner product spaces. But remember: A unitary operator maps a vector space to itself, while an
isometry maps a vector space to another (possibly different) vector space.
Example 7.52 (rotation of R2 ):
Suppose θ ∈ R and S is the operator on F 2 whose matrix M(S) with respect to the standard basis of
F 2 is 

 cos θ − sin θ 



 = M(S).
 sin θ cos θ 
The two columns of this matrix form an orthonormal list in F 2 . Therefore, S is an isometry by the
equivalence of (a) and (e) in Theorem 7.49. Thus S is a unitary operator.
If F = R, then S is the counterclockwise rotation by θ radians around the origin of R2 . So it preserves
norms.
Thm 7.53 (characterizations of unitary operators):
Suppose S ∈ L(V ). Suppose e1 , . . . , en is an orthonormal basis of V . Then
(a) S is a unitary operator.
(b) S ∗ S = SS ∗ = I.
(c) S is invertible and S −1 = S ∗ .
(d) Se1 , . . . , Sen is an orthonormal basis of V .
(e) The rows of M(S, (e1 , . . . , en )) form an orthonormal basis of F n with respect to the Euclidean
inner product.
(f) S ∗ is a unitary operator.
Thm 7.54 (eigenvalues of unitary operators):
Suppose λ is an eigenvalue of a unitary operator. Then |λ|= 1.
Thm 7.55 (description of unitary operators on complex inner product spaces):
Suppose F = C and S ∈ L(V ). Then the following are equivalent.
(a) S is a unitary operator.
(b) There is an orthonormal basis of V consisting of eigenvectors of S whose corresponding eigenvalues all have absolute value 1.

QR Factorization (Sub-Section 7D3)
Def 7.56 (unitary matrix):
An n-by-n matrix is called “unitary” if its columns form an orthonormal list in F n .
Thm 7.57 (characterizations of unitary matrices):
Let Q ∈ F n,n . Then the following are equivalent.
(a) Q is a unitary matrix.
(b) The rows of Q form an orthonormal list in F n .
(c) Qv = ∥v∥

v ∈ F n.

(d) Q∗ Q = QQ∗ = I, the n-by-n matrix with 1’s on the diagonal and 0’s elsewhere.
Thm 7.58 (QR factorization):
Suppose A is a square matrix with linearly independent columns. Then there exist unique matrices
Q and R such that Q is unitary, R is upper triangular with only positive numbers on its diagonal, and
A = QR.

Singular Value Decomposition (Section 7E)

Cholesky Factorization (Sub-Section 7D4)
Thm 7.61 (positive invertible operator):
A self-adjoint operator T ∈ L(V ) is a positive invertible operator ⇐⇒ T v , v > 0

∀v ∈ V , v ̸= 0.

Def 7.62 (positive definite):
A matrix B ∈ F n,n is called “positive definite” if B∗ = B and
Bx , x > 0

∀x ∈ F n , x ̸= 0.

Thm 7.63 (Cholesky factorization):
Suppose B is a positive definite matrix. Then there exists a unique upper triangular matrix R with
only positive numbers on its diagonal such that
B = R∗ R.

Singular Value Decomposition (Section 7E)
Singular Values (Sub-Section 7E1)
Thm 7.64 (properties of T ∗ T ):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). Then
(a) T ∗ T is a positive operator on V ;
(b) null T ∗ T = null T ;
(c) range T ∗ T = range T ∗ ;
(d) dim range T = dim range T ∗ = dim range T ∗ T ;
Def 7.65 (singular values):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). The “singular values” of T are the nonnegative square roots of the eigenvalues
of T ∗ T , listed in decreasing order, each included as many times as the dimension of the corresponding
eigenspace of T ∗ T .
Thm 7.68 (role of positive singular values):
Suppose that T ∈ L(V , W ). Then
(a) T is injective ⇐⇒ 0 is not a singular value of T ;
(b) the number of positive singular values of T equals dim range T ;
(c) T is surjective ⇐⇒ number of positive singular values of T equals dim W .

SVD for Linear Maps and for Matrices (Sub-Section 7E2)
Thm 7.70 (singular value decomposition):
Let T ∈ L(V , W ) and the positive singular values of T are s1 , . . . , sm . Then there exist two orthonormal
lists e1 , . . . , em in V and f1 , . . . , fm in W such that

Def 7.74 (diagonal matrix):
An M-by-N matrix A is called a “diagonal matrix” if all entries of the matrix are 0 except possibly
Ak,k for k ∈ {1, . . . , min{M, N }}.

Thm 7.76 (matrix version of SVD):
Suppose A is a p-by-n matrix of rank m ≥ 1. Then there exists a p-by-m matrix B with orthonormal
columns, an m-by-m diagonal matrix D with positive numbers on the diagonal, and an n-by-m matrix
C with orthonormal columns such that
A = BDC ∗ .

Consequences of Singular Value Decomposition (Section 7F)
Norms of Linear Maps (Sub-Section 7F1)
Thm 7.82 (upper bound for T v ):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). Let s1 be the largest singular value of T . Then
T v ≤ s1 ∥v∥

∀v ∈ V .

Def 7.86 (norm of a linear map):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). Then the “norm” of T , denoted by T , is defined by
o
n
T :≡ max T v v ∈ V and ∥v∥ ≤ 1 .
Thm 7.87 (basic properties of norms of linear maps):
Let T ∈ L(V , W ). Then
(a) T ≥ 0;
(b) T = 0 ⇐⇒ T = 0;
(c) λT = |λ| T

∀λ ∈ F ;

(d) S + T ≤ S + T

∀S ∈ L(V , W );

Thm 7.88 (alternative formulas for T ):
Let T ∈ L(V , W ). Then
(a) T = the largest singular value of T ;
(b) T = max{ T v | v ∈ V and ∥v∥ = 1};
(c) T = the smallest number c such that T v ≤ c ∥v∥

∀v ∈ V .

Thm 7.89 (norm of the adjoint):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). Then T ∗ = T .

Thm 7.93 (polar decomposition):
Suppose T ∈ L(V ). Then there exists a unitary operator S ∈ L(V ) such that
√

Furthermore, if T is invertible, then this unitary operator S is unique.
